**Karachi**[^cite_note-23] is the [capital city](181337.md) of the [Pakistani province](446017.md) of [Sindh](28972.md). It is the [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Pakistan_by_population) in Pakistan and 11th [largest](14649921.md) in the world, with a population of over 20 million. Situated at the [southern tip of the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Pakistan) along the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) coast, Karachi formerly served as the [federal capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Capital_Territory_\(Karachi\)) from 1947 to 1959. Ranked as a [beta-global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city), it is Pakistan's premier industrial and [financial centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_centre), with an estimated GDP of over \$200 billion ([PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity)) as of 2021. Karachi is a major [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_area) and is considered Pakistan's most cosmopolitan city, and among the country's most [linguistically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Pakistan), [ethnically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Pakistan), and [religiously](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Pakistan) diverse regions, as well as one of the country's most progressive and socially liberal cities.[^cite_note-31]

The region has been inhabited for millennia,[^cite_note-32] but the city was formally founded as the fortified village of *Kolachi* as recently as 1729.[^cite_note-Studies_on_Karachi-33][^cite_note-Gayer-2014-34] The settlement –  lying between the [Kohistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohistan_\(Sindh\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Larr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larr_\(Region\)) regions of Sindh –  greatly increased in importance with the arrival of the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) in the mid-19th century following [Sindh's annexation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_conquest_of_Sindh).[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] British administrators embarked on substantial projects to transform the city into a major seaport, and connect it with the [extensive railway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_rail_transport_in_Pakistan) of the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent).[^cite_note-Gayer-2014-34] At the time of Pakistan's independence in 1947, the city was the largest in Sindh with an estimated population of 400,000 people, and a slim [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) majority.[^cite_note-Penguin_Publishing_Group-30][^cite_note-CUP2009-37] Following the [partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India), the city experienced a dramatic shift in population and demography with the arrival of hundreds of thousands of [Muslim immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajir_\(Pakistan\)) from India,[^cite_note-38] coupled with an exodus of nearly all of its Hindu residents.[^cite_note-CUP2009-37] The city experienced rapid economic growth following Pakistan's independence, attracting migrants from throughout the country and other regions in South Asia.[^cite_note-Rowman_&_Littlefield-39] According to the [2023 Census of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Census_of_Pakistan), Karachi's total population was 20.3 million.[^cite_note-dawn.com-40] Karachi is one of the world's fastest-growing cities,[^cite_note-Kotkin-41] and has significant communities representing almost every [ethnic group in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Pakistan). Karachi holds more than two million [Bengali immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengalis_in_Pakistan), a million [Afghan refugees](22663356.md), and up to [400,000 Rohingyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_people_in_Pakistan) from [Myanmar](19457.md).[^cite_note-Falling_back-42][^cite_note-cidcm.umd.edu-43][^cite_note-44]

Karachi is now Pakistan's premier industrial and financial centre. The city has a formal economy estimated to be worth \$190 billion as of 2021, which is the largest in the country.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-Lloyd's-46] Karachi collects 35% of [Pakistan's tax revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Pakistan),[^cite_note-47] and generates approximately 25% of [Pakistan's entire GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Pakistan).[^cite_note-adb-48][^cite_note-coastline-49] Approximately 30% of Pakistani industrial output is from Karachi,[^cite_note-stepmother-50] while Karachi's ports handle approximately 95% of [Pakistan's foreign trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_trade_of_Pakistan).[^cite_note-Rand_Corporation-2014-51] Approximately 90% of the multinational corporations and 100% of the banks operating in Pakistan are headquartered in Karachi.[^cite_note-Rand_Corporation-2014-51] It also serves as a transport hub, and contains Pakistan's two largest seaports, the [Port of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Karachi) and [Port Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Qasim), as well as Pakistan's busiest airport, [Jinnah International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_International_Airport).[^cite_note-52] Karachi is also considered to be Pakistan's fashion capital,[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] and has hosted the annual [Karachi Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Fashion_Week) since 2009.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]

Known as the "City of Lights" in the 1960s and 1970s for its vibrant nightlife,[^cite_note-57] Karachi was beset by sharp ethnic, sectarian, and political conflict in the 1980s with the large-scale arrival of weaponry during the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-violentEnd-58] The city had become well known for its high rates of violent crime, but recorded crimes sharply decreased following a crackdown operation against criminals, the [MQM political party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muttahida_Qaumi_Movement_%E2%80%93_Pakistan), and [Islamist](15012.md) militants, initiated in 2013 by the [Pakistan Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Rangers).[^cite_note-New_York_TImes-59] As a result of the operation, Karachi dropped from being ranked the world's 6th-most dangerous city for crime in 2014, to 128th by 2022.[^cite_note-60]

## Etymology

Modern Karachi was reputedly founded in 1729 as the settlement of *Kolachi-jo-Goth* during the rule of [Kalhora dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalhora_dynasty).[^cite_note-Studies_on_Karachi-33] The new settlement is said to have been named in honour of [Mai Kolachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mai_Kolachi), whose son is said to have slain a man-eating crocodile in the village after his elder brothers had already been killed by it.[^cite_note-Studies_on_Karachi-33] The name *Karachee,* a shortened and corrupted version of the original name *Kolachi-jo-Goth*, was used for the first time in a [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) report from 1742 about a shipwreck near the settlement.[^cite_note-The_Dutch_East_India_Company-61][^cite_note-resources.huygens.knaw\.nl-2015-62]

## History

Main articles: [History of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Karachi) and [Timeline of Karachi history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Karachi_history)

### Early history



The 15th–18th century [Chaukhandi tombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaukhandi_tombs) are a Tentative [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md).

The region around Karachi has been the site of human habitation for millennia. [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic) and [Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic) sites have been excavated in the [Mulri Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulri_Hills) along Karachi's northern outskirts. These earliest inhabitants are believed to have been [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer), with ancient [flint tools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flint_tools) discovered at several sites.

The expansive Karachi region is believed to have been known to the [ancient Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), and may have been the site of [Barbarikon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbarikon), an ancient seaport which was located at the nearby mouth of the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] Karachi may also have been referred to as *Ramya* in ancient Greek texts.[^cite_note-67]

The ancient site of [Krokola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krokola), a natural harbour west of the Indus where [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) sailed his fleet for [Achaemenid Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Assyria), may have been located near the mouth of Karachi's [Malir River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_River),[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] though some believe it was located near [Gizri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gizri).[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] No other natural harbour exists near the mouth of the Indus that could accommodate a large fleet.[^cite_note-73] [Nearchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nearchus), who commanded Alexander's naval fleet, also mentioned a hilly island by the name of [Morontobara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morontobara) and an adjacent flat island named *Bibakta*, which colonial historians identified as Karachi's [Manora Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora,_Karachi) and [Kiamari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keamari_\(locality\)) (or [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Karachi)), respectively, based on Greek descriptions.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] Both areas were island until well into the colonial era, when silting in led to them being connected to the mainland.[^cite_note-77]

In 711 CE, [Muhammad bin Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_bin_Qasim) conquered the [Sindh](28972.md) and Indus Valley and the port of [Debal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debal), from where he launched his forces further into the Indus Valley in 712.[^cite_note-78] Some have identified the port with Karachi, though some argue the location was somewhere between Karachi and the nearby city of [Thatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatta).[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

Under [Mirza Ghazi Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Ghazi_Beg), the [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) administrator of Sindh, the development of coastal Sindh and the [Indus River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River_Delta) was encouraged. Under his rule, fortifications in the region acted as a bulwark against [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language) incursions into [Sindh](28972.md). In 1553–54, [Ottoman](22278.md) [admiral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admiral) [Seydi Ali Reis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seydi_Ali_Reis), mentioned a small port along the Sindh coast by the name of *Kaurashi* which may have been Karachi.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83] The [Chaukhandi tombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaukhandi_tombs) in Karachi's modern suburbs were built around this time between the 15th and 18th centuries.

### Kolachi settlement and the first port



The [Manora Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora_Fort,_Karachi), built in 1797 to defend Karachi, was captured by the British on 3 February 1839 and upgraded 1888–1889.

The first port was established by the [Kalhoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalhora) near Karachi in the mid-18th century, known as Kharak Bander.[^cite_note-84] Nineteenth century Karachi historian [Seth Naomal Hotchand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seth_Naomal_Hotchand) recorded that a small settlement of 20–25 huts existed along the Karachi Harbour that was known as *Dibro*, which was situated along a pool of water known as *Kolachi-jo-Kun*.[^cite_note-85] In 1725, a band of [Baloch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baloch_people) settlers from [Makran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makran) and [Kalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Kalat) had settled in the hamlet after fleeing droughts and tribal feuds.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014b-86]

A new settlement was built in 1729 at the site of *Dibro*, which came to be known as *Kolachi-jo-Goth* ("The village of *Kolachi*).[^cite_note-Studies_on_Karachi-33] The new settlement is said to have been named in honour of [Mai Kolachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mai_Kolachi), a resident of the old settlement whose son is said to have slain a man-eating crocodile.[^cite_note-Studies_on_Karachi-33] Kolachi was about 40 hectares (99 acres) in size, with some smaller fishing villages scattered in its vicinity.[^cite_note-87] The founders of the new fortified settlement were Sindhi [Baniyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bania_\(caste\)),[^cite_note-Gayer-2014b-86] and are said to have arrived from the nearby town of Kharak Bandar after the harbour there silted in 1728 after heavy rains.[^cite_note-88] Kolachi was fortified, and defended with cannons imported from Muscat, [Oman](22316.md). Under the Talpurs, the *Rah-i-Bandar* road was built to connect the city's port to the caravan terminals.[^cite_note-CulturalAssets-89] This road would eventually be further developed by the British into Bandar Road, which was renamed [Muhammad Ali Jinnah Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah_Road).[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91]

The name *Karachee* was used for the first time in a [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) document from 1742, in which a merchant ship *de Ridderkerk* is shipwrecked near the settlement.[^cite_note-The_Dutch_East_India_Company-61][^cite_note-resources.huygens.knaw\.nl-2015-62] In 1770s, Karachi came under the control of the [Khan of Kalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_of_Kalat), which attracted a second wave of Balochi settlers.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014b-86] In 1795, Karachi was annexed by the [Talpurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talpur_dynasty), triggering a third wave of Balochi settlers who arrived from central Sindh and southern Punjab.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014b-86] The Talpurs built the [Manora Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora_Fort,_Karachi) in 1797,[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] which was used to protect Karachi's Harbour from [al-Qasimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qasimi) pirates.[^cite_note-94]

In 1799 or 1800, the founder of the Talpur dynasty, Mir Fateh Ali Khan, allowed the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) under Nathan Crow to establish a trading post in Karachi.[^cite_note-95] He was allowed to build a house for himself in Karachi at that time, but by 1802 was ordered to leave the city.[^cite_note-96] The city continued to be ruled by the Talpurs until it was occupied by forces under the command of [John Keane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Keane,_1st_Baron_Keane) in February 1839.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaurent_Gayer201442-97]

### British control



An 1897 image of Karachi's Rampart Row street in [Mithadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithadar)



Mules Mansion



Some of Karachi's most recognized structures, such as [Frere Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frere_Hall), date from the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj).



Karachi features several examples of colonial-era [Indo-Saracenic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Saracenic_Revival_architecture), such as the [KMC Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Municipal_Corporation_Building).

The [British East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_East_India_Company) captured Karachi on 3 February 1839 after [HMS *Wellesley*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Wellesley_\(1815\)) opened fire and quickly destroyed [Manora Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora_Fort,_Karachi), which guarded Karachi Harbour at [Manora Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora,_Karachi).[^cite_note-98] Karachi's population at the time was an estimated 8,000 to 14,000,[^cite_note-99] and was confined to the walled city in [Mithadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithadar), with suburbs in what is now the [Serai Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serai_Quarter).[^cite_note-CulturalAssets-89] British troops, known as the "Company Bahadur" established a camp to the east of the captured city, which became the precursor to the modern [Karachi Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment). The British further developed the [Karachi Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment) as a military garrison to aid the British war effort in the [First Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War).[^cite_note-Oxford_University_Press-100]

The [Portuguese Goan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Goa) community started migrating to Karachi in the 1820s as traders. The majority of the estimated 100,000 who came to Pakistan are primarily concentrated in Karachi.[^cite_note-101]

Sindh's capital was shifted from [Hyderabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyderabad,_Sindh) to Karachi in 1840 when Karachi was annexed to the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) after Major General [Charles James Napier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_James_Napier) captured the rest of Sindh following his victory against the [Talpurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talpur_dynasty) at the [Battle of Hyderabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hyderabad). Following the 1843 annexation, the entire province was amalgamated into the [Bombay Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Presidency) for the next 93 years, and Karachi remain the divisional headquarter. A few years later in 1846, Karachi suffered a large [cholera](7591.md) outbreak, which led to the establishment of the Karachi Cholera Board (predecessor to the city's civic government).[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102]

The city grew under the administration of its new Commissioner, [Henry Bartle Edward Frere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Bartle_Edward_Frere), who was appointed in the 1850s. Karachi was recognized for its strategic importance, prompting the British to establish the [Port of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Karachi) in 1854. Karachi rapidly became a transportation hub for British India owing to newly built port and rail infrastructure, as well as the increase in agricultural exports from the opening of productive tracts of newly irrigated land in [Punjab](24751.md) and [Sindh](28972.md).[^cite_note-103] By 1856, the value of goods traded through Karachi reached £855,103, leading to the establishment of merchant offices and warehouses.[^cite_note-104] The population in 1856 is estimated to have been 57,000.[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] During the [Sepoy Mutiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepoy_Mutiny) of 1857, the 21st Native Infantry, then stationed in Karachi, mutinied and declared allegiance to rebel forces in September 1857, though the British were able to quickly defeat the rebels and reassert control over the city.

Following the Rebellion, British colonial administrators continued to develop the city's infrastructure, but continued to neglect localities like [Lyari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari), which was home to the city's original population of Sindhi fishermen and Balochi nomads.[^cite_note-Narayanan-2015-106] At the outbreak of the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), Karachi's port became an important cotton-exporting port,[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] with *Indus Steam Flotilla* and *Orient Inland Steam Navigation Company* established to transport cotton from rest of Sindh to Karachi's port, and onwards to textile mills in England.[^cite_note-107] With increased economic opportunities, economic migrants from several ethnicities and religions, including Anglo-British, [Parsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsis), [Marathis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_people), and [Goan Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Goa), among others, established themselves in Karachi,[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] with many setting-up businesses in the new commercial district of [Saddar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddar). [Muhammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah), the founder of Pakistan, was born in Karachi's [Wazir Mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_Mansion_\(Karachi\)) in 1876 to such migrants from [Gujarat](53707.md). Public building works were undertaken at this time in [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture) and [Indo-Saracenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Saracenic_architecture) styles, including the construction of [Frere Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frere_Hall) in 1865 and the later [Empress Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empress_Market) in 1889.

With the completion of the [Suez Canal](29323.md) in 1869, Karachi's position as a major port increased even further.[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] In 1878, the British Raj connected Karachi with the network of [British India's vast railway system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj#Railways). In 1887, [Karachi Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Port) underwent radical improvements with connection to the railways, along with expansion and dredging of the port, and construction of a breakwater.[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] [Karachi's first synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magain_Shalome_Synagogue) was established in 1893.[^cite_note-108] By 1899, Karachi had become the largest wheat-exporting port in the East.[^cite_note-109] In 1901, Karachi's population was 117,000 with a further 109,000 included in the [Municipal area.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Municipal_Committee)[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105]

Under the British, the [city's municipal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Municipal_Committee) was established. Known as the *Father of Modern Karachi*, mayor [Seth Harchandrai Vishandas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seth_Harchandrai_Vishandas) led the municipal government to improve sanitary conditions in the Old City, as well as major infrastructure works in the New Town after his election in 1911.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] In 1914, Karachi had become the largest wheat-exporting port of the entire British Empire,[^cite_note-110] after large irrigation works in [Sindh](28972.md) were initiated to increase wheat and cotton yields.[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] By 1924, the *Drigh Road Aerodrome* was established,[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] now the [Faisal Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PAF_Base_Faisal).

Karachi's increasing importance as a cosmopolitan transportation hub leads to the influence of non-Sindhis in Sindh's administration. Half the city was born outside of Karachi by as early as 1921.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] Native Sindhis were upset by this influence,[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105] and so on 1 April 1936, Sindh was established as a province separate from the Bombay Presidency with Karachi was once again made capital of Sindh. In 1941, the population of the city had risen to 387,000.[^cite_note-Heitzman-2008-105]

### Post-independence



[Lord Mountbatten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Mountbatten,_1st_Earl_Mountbatten_of_Burma) and his wife [Edwina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwina_Mountbatten,_Countess_Mountbatten_of_Burma) in Karachi 14 August 1947

At the dawn of independence following the success of the [Pakistan Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Movement) in 1947, On 15 August 1947 Capital of Sindh shifted from Karachi to Hyderabad and Karachi was made the national capital of Pakistan.

Karachi was Sindh's largest city with a population of over 400,000.[^cite_note-Penguin_Publishing_Group-30] The city had a slight Hindu majority, with around 51% of the population being Hindu. Partition resulted in the exodus of much of the city's Hindu population, though Karachi, like most of Sindh, remained relatively peaceful compared to cities in Punjab.[^cite_note-Markovits-2000-112] Riots erupted on 6 January 1948, after which most of Sindh's Hindu population fled to India,[^cite_note-Markovits-2000-112] with assistance of the Indian government.[^cite_note-113]

Karachi became the focus for the resettlement of middle-class Muslim [*Muhajir*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajir_\(Urdu-speaking_people\)) refugees who fled India, with 470,000 refugees in Karachi by May 1948,[^cite_note-Zamindar-2010-114] leading to a drastic alteration of the [city's demography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Karachi). In 1941, [Muslims](19541.md) were 42% of Karachi's population, but by 1951 made up 96% of the city's population.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] The city's population had tripled between 1941 and 1951.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) replaced [Sindhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_language) as Karachi's most widely spoken language; Sindhi was the mother tongue of 51% of Karachi in 1941, but only 8.5% in 1951, while Urdu grew to become the mother tongue of 51% of Karachi's population.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] 100,000 Muhajir refugees arrived annually in Karachi until 1952. Muhajirs kept arriving from different parts of India until 2000.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111]

Karachi was selected as the first capital of Pakistan, and was administered as a federal district separate from Sindh beginning in 1948,[^cite_note-Zamindar-2010-114] the capital of Sindh shifted again from Hyderabad to Karachi until the national capital was shifted to [Rawalpindi](343524.md) in 1958.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarbara_A._Weightman2011\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_February_2023\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (February_2023)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-115] While foreign embassies shifted away from Karachi, [the city is host to](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_missions_in_Karachi) numerous [consulates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consul_\(representative\)) and honorary consulates.[^cite_note-116] Between 1958 and 1970, Karachi's role as capital of Sindh was ceased due to the [One Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Unit) programme enacted by President [Iskander Mirza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iskander_Mirza).[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1]

Karachi of the 1960s was regarded as an economic role model around the world, with [Seoul](19159283.md), South Korea, borrowing from the city's second "Five-Year Plan".[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] Several examples of [Modernist architect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernist_architect) were built in Karachi during this period, including the [Mazar-e-Quaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-e-Quaid) mausoleum, the distinct [Masjid-e-Tooba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_e_Tooba), and the [Habib Bank Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habib_Bank_Plaza) (the tallest building in all of South Asia at the time). The city's population by 1961 had grown 369% compared to 1941.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] By the mid-1960s, Karachi began to attract large numbers of [Pashtun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns), [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Muslims) and [Kashmiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris) from northern Pakistan.[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111]

The 1970s saw a construction boom funded by remittances and investments from the [Gulf States](3088555.md), and the appearance of apartment buildings in the city.[^cite_note-119] Real-estate prices soared during this period, leading to a worsening housing crisis.[^cite_note-120] The period also saw [labour unrest in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_labour_unrest_of_1972)'s industrial estates beginning in 1970 that were violently repressed by the government of President [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto) from 1972 onwards.[^cite_note-121] To appease conservative forces, Bhutto banned alcohol in Pakistan, and cracked-down of Karachi's discotheques and cabarets – leading to the closure of Karachi's once-lively nightlife.[^cite_note-Udupa-2017-122] The city's art scene was further repressed during the rule of dictator [General Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq).[^cite_note-Udupa-2017-122] Zia's Islamization policies lead the Westernized upper-middle classes of Karachi to largely withdraw from the public sphere, and instead form their own social venues that became inaccessible to the poor.[^cite_note-Udupa-2017-122] This decade also saw an influx of more than one million [Bihari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bihari_Muslims) immigrants into Karachi from the newly made country [Bangladesh](3454.md) which separated from Pakistan in 1971.

In 1972, the [Karachi district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_district) divided into three districts, [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_East_District), [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_West_District) and [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_South_District) districts.



Karachi street scene in 1987

The 1980s and 1990s saw an influx of almost one million [Afghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_people) refugees into Karachi fleeing the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] This was followed by refugees escaping from post-revolution [Iran](14653.md).[^cite_note-123] At this time, Karachi was also rocked by political conflict, while crime rates drastically increased with the arrival of weaponry from the [War in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(1978%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-violentEnd-58] Conflict between the [MQM party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muttahida_Qaumi_Movement_%E2%80%93_Pakistan), and ethnic [Sindhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhis), [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns), [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabis) and [Balochis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochis) was sharp.[^cite_note-124] The party and its vast network of supporters were targeted by Pakistani security forces as part of the controversial [Operation Clean-up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Clean-up) in 1992 – an effort to restore peace in the city that lasted until 1994.[^cite_note-125] Anti-Hindu riots also broke out in Karachi in 1992 in retaliation for the demolition of the [Babri Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babri_Mosque) in India by a group of Hindu nationalists earlier that year.[^cite_note-126]

In 1996, two (02) more districts created in the [Karachi Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Division) named [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Central_District) and [Malir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_District) districts.

The 2010s saw another influx of hundreds of thousands of Pashtun refugees fleeing [conflict in North-West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_North-West_Pakistan) and the [2010 Pakistan floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Pakistan_floods).[^cite_note-Gayer-2014a-111] By this point Karachi had become widely known for its high rates of violent crime, usually in relation to criminal activity, gang-warfare, sectarian violence, and extrajudicial killings.[^cite_note-Narayanan-2015-106] Recorded crimes sharply decreased following a controversial crackdown operation against criminals, the MQM party, and Islamist militants initiated in 2013 by the [Pakistan Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Rangers).[^cite_note-New_York_TImes-59] As a result of the operation, Karachi went from being ranked the world's 6th most dangerous city for crime in 2014, to 128th by 2022.[^cite_note-127]

In 2022 at least one million [flood affectees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Pakistan_floods) from [Sindh](28972.md) and [Balochistan](8431743.md) took refuge in Karachi.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

A major fire broke out on 18 January 2026 at Gul Plaza, a large shopping mall in Karachi, Pakistan, killing at least 21 people and leaving more than 60 missing. The blaze, one of the city’s largest in over a decade, spread through the multi-story complex containing around 1,200 shops and burned for more than 24 hours before being mostly extinguished. Rescue teams later recovered bodies from the site while cranes demolished remaining structures amid collapse concerns. The fire caused widespread damage, with many shop owners losing years of investment and livelihoods.[^cite_note-130]

## Geography



Karachi (left) in comparison to the [Indus River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River_Delta)

Main articles: [Geography of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Karachi) and [Environment of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Karachi)

Karachi is located on the coastline of Sindh province in southern Pakistan, along the [Karachi Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Harbour), a natural harbour on the [Arabian Sea](2747.md). Karachi is built on a coastal plain with scattered rocky outcroppings, hills and marshlands. [Mangrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove) forests grow in the brackish waters around the Karachi Harbour (see: [Chinna Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinna_Creek)), and farther southeast towards the expansive [Indus River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River_Delta). West of Karachi city is the [Cape Monze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Monze), locally known as [Ras Muari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_Muari), which is an area characterised by sea cliffs, rocky sandstone promontories and beaches.

Karachi lies very close to a major fault line, where the [Indian tectonic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate) meets the [Arabian tectonic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Plate).[^cite_note-131] However, Karachi lies near the western edge of the Indian Plate, on the Indo Gangetic Plain. Within the city of Karachi are two small ranges: the [Khasa Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khasa_Hills) and [Mulri Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulri_Hills), which lie in the northwest and act as a barrier between [North Nazimabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Nazimabad) and [Orangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orangi).[^cite_note-132] Karachi's hills are barren and are part of the larger [Kirthar Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirthar_Range), and have a maximum elevation of 528 metres (1,732 feet).[^cite_note-IIED-133]

Between the hills are wide coastal plains interspersed with dry river beds and water channels. Karachi has developed around the [Malir River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_River) and [Lyari Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari_River), with the Lyari shore being the site of the settlement for *Kolachi*. To the east of Karachi lies the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) flood plains.[^cite_note-UCL-134]

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Karachi)



The [Arabian Sea](2747.md) influences Karachi's climate, providing the city with more moderate temperatures compared to other areas of [Sindh](28972.md) province.

Karachi has a tropical [semi arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi_arid) climate ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *BSh*), formerly a [desert climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert_climate), dominated by a long "Summer Season" while moderated by oceanic influence from the [Arabian Sea](2747.md). The city has annual average precipitation levels (approx. 296 mm (12 in) per annum), the bulk of which occurs during the late June–September [monsoon](57630.md) season. Summers are hot and humid, and Karachi is prone to deadly heatwaves. Over the past 20 years, rainfall has become more abundant. Tropical storms and thunderstorms, as well as flooding are becoming more common, especially during the summer monsoon.[^cite_note-135] On the other hand, cool sea breezes typically provide relief during hot summer months. A text message-based early warning system alerts people to take precautionary measures and helps prevent fatalities during an unusually strong heatwave or thunderstorm.[^cite_note-heatwave-136] The winter climate is dry and lasts between December and February. It is dry and pleasant in winter relative to the warm hot season that follows, which starts in March and lasts until October. Proximity to the sea maintains humidity levels at near-constant levels year-round. Thus, the climate is similar to a humid tropical climate, except for the low precipitation and occasional temperatures well over 100 F (38 C) due to the influence of the Thar Desert nearby, close to the border with India.

The city's highest annual rainfall was about 750–850 mm, recorded in the late 1970s. The city's highest monthly rainfall, 19 in (480 mm), occurred in July 1967.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-pakmet-138] The city's highest rainfall in 24 hours occurred on 7 August 1953, when about 278.1 millimetres (10.95 in) of rain lashed the city, resulting in major flooding.[^cite_note-139]

Karachi's highest recorded temperature is 48.0 °C (118.4 °F) which was recorded on 22 and 23 April 2017,[^cite_note-140] and the lowest is 0 °C (32 °F) recorded on 21 January 1934.[^cite_note-pakmet-138]

### Cityscape



Glimpse of [I. I. Chundrigar Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._I._Chundrigar_Road), as captured from the southern vantage point overlooking [Chinna Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinna_Creek).

The city first developed around the Karachi Harbour, and owes much of its growth to its role as a seaport at the end of the 18th century,[^cite_note-146] contrasted with Pakistan's millennia-old cities such as [Lahore](125315.md), [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan), and [Peshawar](66230.md). Karachi's [Mithadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithadar) neighbourhood represents the extent of *Kolachi* prior to British rule.

British Karachi was divided between the "New Town" and the "Old Town", with British investments focused primarily on the New Town.[^cite_note-Oxford_University_Press-100] The Old Town was a largely unplanned neighbourhood which housed most of the city's indigenous residents and had no access to sewerage systems, electricity, and water.[^cite_note-Oxford_University_Press-100] The New Town was subdivided into residential, commercial, and military areas.[^cite_note-Oxford_University_Press-100] Given the strategic value of the city, the British developed the [Karachi Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment) as a military garrison in the New Town to aid the British war effort in the [First Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War).[^cite_note-Oxford_University_Press-100] The city's development was largely confined to the area north of the [Chinna Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinna_Creek) prior to independence, although the seaside area of [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Karachi) was also developed as a posh locale under the British, and its large bungalows and estates remain some of the city's most desirable properties. The aforementioned historic areas form the oldest portions of Karachi, and contain its most important monuments and government buildings, with the [I. I. Chundrigar Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._I._Chundrigar_Road) being home to most of Pakistan's banks, including the Habib Bank Plaza which was Pakistan's tallest building from 1963 until the early 2000s.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] Situated on a coastal plain northwest of Karachi's historic core lies the sprawling district of [Orangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orangi). North of the historic core is the largely middle-class district of [Nazimabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazimabad), and upper-middle-class [North Nazimabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Nazimabad), which were developed in the 1950s. To the east of the historic core is the area known as [Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Housing_Authority,_Karachi), an expansive upscale suburb developed and administered by the [Pakistan Army](1965925.md). Karachi's coastal plains along the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) south of Clifton were also developed much later as part of the greater Defence Housing Authority project. Karachi's city limits also include several islands, including [Baba and Bhit Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_and_Bhit_Islands), Oyster Rocks, and [Manora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora,_Karachi), a former island which is now connected to the mainland by a thin 12-kilometre long [shoal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoal) known as [Sandspit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandspit_Beach).[^cite_note-IIED-133] [Gulistan-e-Johar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulistan-e-Johar), [Gulshan-e-Iqbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulshan_Town), [Federal B. Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_B._Area), [Malir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir), [Landhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landhi_Town) and [Korangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korangi_Town) areas were all developed after 1970. The city has been described as one divided into sections for those able to afford to live in planned localities with access to urban amenities, and those who live in unplanned communities with inadequate access to such services.[^cite_note-Askari-2015-147] 35% of Karachi's residents live in unplanned communities.[^cite_note-Askari-2015-147]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Karachi)



[Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Karachi) is considered as one of the richest neighbourhoods in Pakistan.

Being the largest city, Karachi is also Pakistan's financial and commercial capital.[^cite_note-148] Since Pakistan's independence, Karachi has been the centre of the nation's economy, and remain's Pakistan's largest urban economy despite the economic stagnation caused by sociopolitical unrest during the late 1980s and 1990s. The city forms the centre of an economic corridor stretching from Karachi to nearby [Hyderabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyderabad,_Sindh), and [Thatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatta).[^cite_note-Pakistan_Development_Update-2016-149]

As of 2021, Karachi had an estimated GDP (PPP) of \$190 billion with a yearly growth rate of 5.5%.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-Lloyd's-46] Karachi contributes 90% of Sindh's GDP[^cite_note-gdp4-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] and accounts for approximately 25% of the total GDP of Pakistan.[^cite_note-adb-48][^cite_note-coastline-49] The city has a large [informal economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy) which is not typically reflected in GDP estimates.[^cite_note-155] The informal economy may constitute up to 36% of Pakistan's total economy, versus 22% of India's economy, and 13% of the Chinese economy.[^cite_note-156] The informal sector employs up to 70% of the city's workforce.[^cite_note-157] In 2018 The Global Metro Monitor Report ranked Karachi's economy as the best performing metropolitan economy in Pakistan.[^cite_note-158]



[I. I. Chundrigar Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._I._Chundrigar_Road) is considered to be the "downtown" of karachi



Under construction high rises in DHA Karachi

Today along with Pakistan's continued economic expansion Karachi is now ranked third in the world for consumer expenditure growth with its market anticipated to increase by 6.6% in real terms in 2018[^cite_note-159] It is also ranked among the top cities in the world by an anticipated increase of a number of households (1.3 million households) with annual income above \$20,000 measured at PPP exchange rates by 2025.[^cite_note-160] The Global FDI Intelligence Report 2017/2018 published by [Financial Times](136566.md) ranks Karachi amongst the top 10 Asia pacific cities of the future for FDI strategy.[^cite_note-161] According to Anatol Lieven the economic growth of Karachi is a result of the influx of [Muhajirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahajir_\(Pakistan\)) to Karachi during late 1940s and early 50s.[^cite_note-162]

### Finance and banking

Most of Pakistan's public and private banks are headquartered on Karachi's [I. I. Chundrigar Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._I._Chundrigar_Road), which is known as "Pakistan's Wall Street",[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] with a large percentage of the cash flow in the Pakistani economy taking place on I. I. Chundrigar Road. Most major foreign [multinational corporations](214491.md) operating in Pakistan have their headquarters in Karachi. Karachi is also home to the [Pakistan Stock Exchange](49066780.md), which was rated as Asia's best-performing stock market in 2015 on the heels of Pakistan's upgrade to emerging-market status by [MSCI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MSCI).[^cite_note-163]

### Media and technology

Main articles: [Media in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Karachi), [Cinema in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_in_Karachi), [List of television stations in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_stations_in_Karachi), [List of magazines in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_magazines_in_Karachi), and [List of newspapers in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Karachi)

Karachi has been the pioneer in cable networking in Pakistan with the most sophisticated of the cable networks of any city of Pakistan,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmos_Owen_Thomas2005121-164] and has seen an expansion of [information and communications technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology) and [electronic media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_media). The city has become a software outsourcing hub for Pakistan. Several independent [television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Pakistan) and radio stations are based in Karachi, including [Business Plus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Plus_\(TV_Channel\)), [AAJ News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AAJ_TV), [Geo TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jang_Media_Group), [KTN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawish_Television_Network),[^cite_note-165] [Sindh TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindh_TV),[^cite_note-166] [CNBC Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNBC_Pakistan), TV ONE, [Express TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Express_News_\(Pakistan\)),[^cite_note-167] [ARY Digital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARY_Digital), Indus Television Network, [Samaa TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaa_TV), [Abb Takk News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abb_Takk_News), [Bol TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BOL_Network), and [Dawn News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawn_News), as well as several local stations.

### Industry

Industry contributes a large portion of Karachi's economy, with the city home to several of Pakistan's largest companies dealing in textiles, cement, steel, heavy machinery, chemicals, and food products.[^cite_note-168] The city is home to approximately 30 percent of Pakistan's manufacturing sector,[^cite_note-stepmother-50] and produces approximately 42 percent of Pakistan's [value added](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_added) in large scale manufacturing.[^cite_note-169] At least 4500 industrial units form Karachi's formal industrial economy.[^cite_note-UCL-134] Karachi's informal manufacturing sector employs far more people than the formal sector, though proxy data suggest that the capital employed and value-added from such informal enterprises is far smaller than that of formal sector enterprises.[^cite_note-170] An estimated 63% of the Karachi's workforce is employed in trade and manufacturing.[^cite_note-Pakistan_Development_Update-2016-149]

Karachi Export Processing Zone, SITE, [Korangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korangi), Northern Bypass Industrial Zone, [Bin Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bin_Qasim,_Karachi) and North Karachi serve as large industrial estates in Karachi.[^cite_note-171] The [Karachi Expo Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Expo_Centre) also complements Karachi's industrial economy by hosting regional and international exhibitions.[^cite_note-172]

### Revenue collection



The former State Bank of Pakistan building was built during the colonial era.

As home to Pakistan's largest ports and a large portion of its manufacturing base, Karachi contributes a large share of Pakistan's collected tax revenue. As most of Pakistan's large multinational corporations are based in Karachi, income taxes are paid in the city even though income may be generated from other parts of the country.[^cite_note-184] As home to the country's two largest ports, Pakistani customs officials collect the bulk of federal duty and tariffs at Karachi's ports, even if those imports are destined for one of Pakistan's other provinces.[^cite_note-185] Approximately 25% of Pakistan's national revenue is *generated* in Karachi.[^cite_note-adb-48]

According to the [Federal Board of Revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Board_of_Revenue)'s 2006–2007 year book, tax and customs units in Karachi were responsible for 46.75% of direct taxes, 33.65% of federal excise tax, and 23.38% of domestic sales tax.[^cite_note-fbr0607-186] Karachi accounts for 75.14% of customs duty and 79% of sales tax on imports,[^cite_note-fbr0607-186] and collects 53.38% of the total collections of the Federal Board of Revenue, of which 53.33% are customs duty and sales tax on imports.[^cite_note-fbr0607-186][^cite_note-187]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Karachi), [Ethnic groups in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Karachi), and [Religion in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Karachi)



Bahadurabad Area has a high population density.

Karachi has an overall plurality of [Urdu-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu-speaking) [Muhajirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajir_\(Pakistan\)), followed by [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns) as the second largest group, while [Sindhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhis) are the third largest, followed by [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabis) with over a million people each respectively. Along with other large communities of [Balochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochs), [Saraikis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saraikis), and [Hindkowans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindkowans), there are also smaller communities of [Brahuis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahuis), [Kashmiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris), [Shina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shina_people), [Balti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balti_people), [Gujaratis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujaratis), [Memons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memons), [Bohras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohras), [Kutchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutchi_people), [Kalasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalasha), and many more coexisting.[^cite_note-PBoS23-188] It is the most linguistically, ethnically, and religiously diverse city in Pakistan.[^cite_note-Penguin_Publishing_Group-30] The city is a [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot) of ethnolinguistic groups from throughout Pakistan, as well as migrants from other parts of Asia. The [2017 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Census_of_Pakistan) numerated Karachi's population to be 14,910,352, having grown 2.49% per year since the 1998 census, which had listed Karachi's population at approximately 9.3 million.[^cite_note-ETT-189] According to the [2023 Census of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Census_of_Pakistan), Karachi's total population was 20.3 million.[^cite_note-dawn.com-40] Karachi is one of the world's fastest-growing cities,[^cite_note-Kotkin-41] The city's inhabitants are referred to by the [demonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym) *Karachiite* in English, and *Karāchīwālā* in Urdu.

### Population

At the end of the 19th century, Karachi had an estimated population of 105,000. By the dawn of [Pakistan's independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Pakistan\)) in 1947, the city had an estimated population of 400,000.[^cite_note-Penguin_Publishing_Group-30] The city's population grew dramatically with the arrival of hundreds of thousands of [Muslim refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajir_\(Urdu-speaking_people\)) from the newly independent [Republic of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_India).[^cite_note-197] Rapid economic growth following independence attracted further migrants from throughout Pakistan and South Asia.[^cite_note-Rowman_&_Littlefield-39] The [2017 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Census_of_Pakistan) numerated Karachi's population to be 14,910,352, having grown 2.49% per year since the 1998 census, which had listed Karachi's population at approximately 9.3 million.[^cite_note-ETT-189]

Lower than expected population figures from the census suggest that Karachi's poor infrastructure, law and order situation, and weakened economy relative to other parts of Pakistan made the city less attractive to in-migration than previously thought.[^cite_note-ETT-189] The figure is disputed by all the major political parties in [Sindh](28972.md).[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] Karachi's population grew by 59.8% since the 1998 census to 14.9 million, while [Lahore](125315.md) city grew 75.3%[^cite_note-stats-201] – though Karachi's census district had not been altered by the provincial government since 1998, while Lahore's had been expanded by Punjab's government,[^cite_note-stats-201] leading to some of Karachi's growth to have occurred outside the city's census boundaries.[^cite_note-ETT-189] Karachi's population had grown at a rate of 3.49% between the 1981 and 1998 census, leading many analysts to estimate Karachi's 2017 population to be approximately 18 million by extrapolating a continued annual growth rate of 3.49%. Some had expected that the city's population to be between 22 and 30 million,[^cite_note-ETT-189] which would require an annual growth rate accelerating to between 4.6% and 6.33%.[^cite_note-ETT-189]

### Ethnicity

The oldest portions of modern Karachi reflect the ethnic composition of the first settlement, with Sindhis and Balochis continuing to make up a large portion of the Lyari neighbourhood, though many of the residents are relatively recent migrants. Following Partition, large numbers of Hindus left Pakistan for the newly independent [Dominion of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_India) (later the Republic of India), while a larger percentage of Muslim migrant and refugees from India settled in Karachi. The city grew 150% during the ten-year period between 1941 and 1951 with the new arrivals from India,[^cite_note-210] who made up 57% of Karachi's population in 1951.[^cite_note-211] The city is now considered a melting pot of Pakistan and is the country's most diverse city.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1]

In 2011, an estimated 2.5 million foreign migrants lived in the city, mostly from [Afghanistan](737.md), [Bangladesh](3454.md), [Myanmar](19457.md), and [Sri Lanka](26750.md).[^cite_note-212]



Karachi is home to large numbers of descendants of refugees and migrants from [Hyderabad](37534.md), in southern India, who built a small replica of Hyderabad's famous [Charminar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charminar) monument in Karachi's [Bahadurabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahadurabad) area.

Much of Karachi's citizenry descend from Urdu-speaking migrants and refugees from North India who became known by the Arabic term for "Migrant": [Muhajir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajir_\(Urdu-speaking_people\)). The first Muhajirs of Karachi arrived in 1946 in the aftermath of the [Direct Action Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_Action_Day) and subsequent [1946 Bihar riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_Bihar_riots).[^cite_note-213] The city's wealthy Hindus opposed the resettlement of refugees near their homes, and so many refugees were accommodated in the older and more congested parts of Karachi.[^cite_note-214] The city witnessed a large influx of Muhajirs following partition, who were drawn to the port city and newly designated federal capital for its white-collar job opportunities.[^cite_note-215] Muhajirs continued to migrate to Pakistan throughout the 1950s and early 1960s,[^cite_note-Khalidi-1998-216] with Karachi remaining the primary destination of Indian Muslim migrants throughout those decades.[^cite_note-Khalidi-1998-216] The Muhajir Urdu-speaking community in the 2017 census forms slightly less than 45% of the city's population.[^cite_note-stats-201]

Karachi is home to a wide array of non-Urdu speaking Muslim peoples from what is now the [Republic of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_India). The city has a sizable community of [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_Muslims), [Marathi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_Muslims), [Konkani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konkani_Muslims)-speaking refugees.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] Karachi is also home to a several-thousand member strong community of [Malabari Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malabar_Muslims) from [Kerala](4349459.md) in [South India](678583.md).[^cite_note-217] These ethno-linguistic groups are being [assimilated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) in the Urdu-speaking community.[^cite_note-218]

During the period of rapid economic growth in the 1960s, large numbers [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_people) from the [NWFP](21950.md) migrated to Karachi with Afghan Pashtun refugees settling in Karachi during the 80's.,[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] Pashtuns from Afghanistan are regarded as the most conservative community.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] The Pashtun community forms the bulk of manual labourers and transporters.

The [Sindhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhis) makeup around 11–12 percent of Karachi's population, Sindhis form much of the municipal and provincial bureaucracy.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] Being the Provincial capital of [Sindh](28972.md) Province, Karachi attracts many Sindhis from other parts of [Sindh](28972.md). In recent years Karachi has seen massive [Sindhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhis) migration to the city due to being economic and commercial hub as well as Capital of the province, While mostly Sindhis of Karachi are [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_Muslims) , The bulk of Karachi's [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) community, which makes up 1.12% of the city's population, is Sindhi.

Migrants from Punjab began settling in Karachi in large numbers in the 1960s, and now make up an estimated 8% of Karachi's population.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] The bulk of Karachi's Christian community, which makes up 2.5% of the city's population, is Punjabi.

4% of Karachi's population speaks Balochi as its mother tongue, though most Baloch speakers are of [Sheedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheedi) heritage – a community that traces its roots to Africa.

Following the [Indo-Pakistani War of 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971) and independence of [Bangladesh](3454.md), thousands of Urdu-speaking [Biharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bihari_people) arrived in the city, preferring to remain Pakistani rather than live in the newly independent country. Large numbers of [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_people) also migrated from Bangladesh to Karachi during periods of economic growth in the 1980s and 1990s. Karachi is now home to an estimated 2.5 to 3 million ethnic [Bengalis living in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshis_in_Pakistan).[^cite_note-Falling_back-42][^cite_note-cidcm.umd.edu-43] [Rohingya](1918632.md) refugees from [Myanmar](19457.md), who speak a dialect of Bengali and are sometimes regarded as Bengalis, also live in the city. Karachi is home to an estimated [400,000 Rohingya residents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese_people_in_Pakistan).[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] Large scale [Rohingya](1918632.md) migration to Karachi made Karachi one of the largest population centres of [Rohingyas](1918632.md) in the world outside of Myanmar.[^cite_note-226]

Central Asian migrants from [Uzbekistan](31853.md) and [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md) have also settled in the city.[^cite_note-conflictedkarachi-227] Domestic workers from the [Philippines](23440.md) are employed in Karachi's posh locales, while many of the city's teachers hail from [Sri Lanka](26750.md).[^cite_note-conflictedkarachi-227] Many Sri Lankans moved to Karachi due to the 2022 [Economic Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%93present_Sri_Lankan_economic_crisis) in Sri Lanka. Expatriates from China began migrating to Karachi in the 1940s, to work as dentists, chefs and shoemakers, while many of their descendants continue to live in Pakistan.[^cite_note-conflictedkarachi-227][^cite_note-228] Chinese also reached Karachi after 2015 in large number due to the [CPEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CPEC) project. The city is also home to a small number of British and American expatriates.[^cite_note-229]

During [World War II](32927.md), about 3,000 Polish refugees from the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), with some Polish families who chose to remain in the city after Partition.[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] Post-Partition Karachi also once had a sizable refugee community from post-revolutionary [Iran](14653.md).[^cite_note-conflictedkarachi-227]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Karachi)

| **Religions** |  | **Percent** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Islam](6037917.md) |  | 96.53% |
| Christianity |  | 2.21% |
| [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) |  | 1.12% |
| Others |  | 0.14% |



With a capacity of 800,000 worshippers, [Grand Jamia Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Jamia_Mosque,_Karachi) is the largest mosque in Pakistan and 3rd largest in the world.



[St. Patrick's Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Patrick's_Cathedral,_Karachi), built-in 1881, serves as the seat of the [Archdiocese of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Karachi).



The [Swaminarayan Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shri_Swaminarayan_Mandir,_Karachi) is the largest [Hindu temple](2247213.md) in Karachi.

Karachi is a religiously homogeneous city with more than 96 per cent of its population adhering to Islam.[^cite_note-233] *Karachiites* adhere to numerous sects and sub-sects of [Islam](6037917.md), as well as [Protestant Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Christianity), and community of [Goan Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goan_Catholics). The city also is home to large numbers of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu), and a small community of [Zoroastrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrian) and [Parsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsi). According to Nichola Khan Karachi is also the world's largest Muslim city.[^cite_note-234] Prior to Pakistan's independence in 1947, the religious demographics of the city was estimated to be 51.1% Hindu, 42.3% Muslim, with the remaining 7% primarily Christians (both British and native), Sikhs, Jains, with a small number of Jews.[^cite_note-235] Following the independence of Pakistan, the vast majority of Karachi's [Sindhi Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_Hindus) population left for India while Muslim refugees from India, in turn, settled in the city. This mass migration dramatically changed the religious demographics of the city.
