#### Islam

Main article: [Islam in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Karachi)

[Karachi is overwhelmingly Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Karachi), though the city is one of Pakistan's most secular cities.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1][^cite_note-India_Today-25][^cite_note-dw\.com-26] Approximately 85% of Karachi's Muslims are [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam), while 15% are [Shi'ites](26961.md).[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) primarily follow the [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) school of [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence), with [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) influencing religious practices by encouraging reverence for Sufi saints such as [Abdullah Shah Ghazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Shah_Ghazi) and [Mewa Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mewa_Shah). [Shi'ites](26961.md) are predominantly [Twelver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver), with a significant [Ismaili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailism) minority which is further subdivided into [Nizaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizari), [Mustaalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustaali), [Dawoodi Bohras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawoodi_Bohra), and [Sulaymanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulaymani). There are over 3000 mosques in Karachi, most famous of which include [Grand Jamia Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Jamia_Mosque,_Karachi), [Baitul Mukarram Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baitul_Mukarram_Mosque), [Masjid-e-Tooba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid-e-Tooba) and [Memon Masjid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Memon_Masjid).

#### Christianity

Approximately 2.2% of Karachi's population is Christian.[^cite_note-Census2023B-250] The city's Christian community is primarily composed of [Punjabi Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Christians) and a community of [Goan Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goan_Catholics) who are typically better-educated and more affluent than their Punjabi co-religionists.[^cite_note-254] They established the posh [Cincinnatus Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnatus_Town) in [Garden East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_East) as a Goan enclave. The Goan community dates from 1820 and has a population estimated to be 12,000–15,000 strong.[^cite_note-255] Karachi is served by its own archdiocese, the [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Karachi).

#### Hinduism



[Shri Laxmi Narayan Mandir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shri_Laxmi_Narayan_Mandir)



[Shri Swami Narayan Mandir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shri_Swaminarayan_Mandir,_Karachi)

While most of the city's Hindu population left *en masse* for India following Pakistan's independence, Karachi still has a large Hindu community with a population of 211,138 forming 1.12% as per 2023 Census.[^cite_note-256] The Hindu community is split into a more affluent [Sindhi Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_Hindu) and small [Punjabi Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Hindus) group that forms part of Karachi's educated middle class, while poorer Hindus of [Rajasthani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajasthani_people) and [Marwari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marwari_people) descent form the other part and typically serve as menial and day laborers. Wealthier Hindus live primarily in [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Karachi) and [Saddar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddar_Town), while poorer ones live and have temples in [Narayanpura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narayan_Pura) and [Lyari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari). There is a small community of [Tamil Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamils_in_Pakistan) in Karachi, with about 100 families in [Madrasi Para](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasi_Para).[^cite_note-257] Many streets in central Karachi still retain Hindu names, especially in [Mithadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithadar), [Aram Bagh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram_Bagh,_Karachi) (formerly Ram Bagh), and Ramswami. The [Panchmukhi Hanuman Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchmukhi_Hanuman_Temple) in [Soldier Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soldier_Bazaar) is believed to be at least 1,500 years old and is a national heritage under the Sindh Cultural Heritage (Preservation) Act 1994.[^cite_note-258]

#### Zoroastrianism

Karachi's affluent and influential [Parsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrian) have lived in the region in the 12th century, though the modern community dates from the mid 19th century when they served as military contractors and commissariat agents to the British.[^cite_note-259] Further waves of Parsi immigrants from [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia) settled in the city in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-260] The population of Parsis in Karachi and throughout South Asia is in continuous decline due to low birth-rates and migration to Western countries.[^cite_note-261]

In 2023, according to the [2023 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Pakistani_census), approximately 1,435 [Parsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsis) are left in Karachi.[^cite_note-262]

## Transportation

Main article: [Transport in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Karachi)



Greenline Metrobus Karachi

### Road

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of streets in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_streets_in_Karachi).

Karachi is served by a road network estimated to be approximately 15,500 kilometres (9,600 miles) in length,[^cite_note-263] serving approximately 5 million vehicles per day.

Karachi is served by 6 Signal-Free Corridors which are designed as urban express roads to permit traffic to transverse large distances without the need to stop at intersections and stoplights. The 16 km (10 mi) Karsaz Road connects [PAF Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Air_Force_Museum) in central Karachi to [SITE Industrial Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SITE_Town). The Rashid Minhas Road connects [Surjani Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surjani_Town) with [Shah Faisal Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Faisal_Town) over a 20 km span. The 19 km (12 mi) University Road connects Karachi's urban centre to the [Gulistan-e-Johar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulistan-e-Johar) suburb. The 18 km (11 mi) [Shahrah-e-Faisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahrah-e-Faisal) connects Karachi's [Sadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddar_Town) area to the [Jinnah International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_International_Airport). The 18 km (11 mi) Shahrah-e-Pakistan connects city centre to [Federal B. Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_B._Area). The 18 km (11 mi) Sher Shah Suri Road connects the city centre to [Nazimabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazimabad).

The [Lyari Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari_Expressway) is a 16 km [controlled-access highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway) along the [Lyari River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari_River). This toll highway is designed to relieve congestion within the city. To the north of Karachi lies the 39 km [Karachi Northern Bypass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Northern_Bypass) (M10), which bypasses the city to connect the M-9 Motorway to the [N25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Highways_of_Pakistan) National Highway. A 39 km (24 mi) Malir Expressway is under construction along the [Malir River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_River). It will link Karachi's [DHA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Housing_Authority) to Karachi's [Malir Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_Town) and terminate at Kathore on the M-9 motorway.

Karachi is the terminus of the [M-9 motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M-9_motorway_\(Pakistan\)), which connects Karachi to [Hyderabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyderabad,_Sindh). The M-9 motorway is part of a larger countrywide motorways network, many of which were built through the [China Pakistan Economic Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Pakistan_Economic_Corridor) Project. From Hyderabad, motorways provide high-speed road access to all major Pakistani cities, including [Peshawar](66230.md), [Islamabad](51153.md), [Lahore](125315.md), [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan) and [Faisalabad](401880.md).

Karachi is also the terminus of the [N-5 National Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-5_National_Highway) which connects the city to the historic medieval capital of Sindh, [Thatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatta). It offers further connections to northern Pakistan and the Afghan border near [Torkham](15844053.md). The N-25 National Highway connects Karachi to [Quetta](93764.md), the capital of Balochistan. The N-10 National Highway connects Karachi to the emerging port city of [Gwadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwadar).

### Rail

Main articles: [Karachi Circular Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Circular_Railway) and [List of railway stations in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railway_stations_in_Pakistan)

Karachi is linked by rail to the rest of the country by [Pakistan Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Railways). The [Karachi City Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_City_Station) and [Karachi Cantonment Railway Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment_Railway_Station) are the city's two major railway stations.[^cite_note-Paracha-2014-1] The city has an international rail link, the [Thar Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thar_Express) which links Karachi Cantonment Station with [Bhagat Ki Kothi station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Ki_Kothi_railway_station) in [Jodhpur](983053.md), India.[^cite_note-264]

The railway system also handles freight linking Karachi port to destinations up-country in northern Pakistan.[^cite_note-265] The city is the terminus for the [Main Line-1 Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi%E2%80%93Peshawar_Line) which connects Karachi to Peshawar. Pakistan's rail network, including the Main Line-1 Railway is being upgraded as part of the [China Pakistan Economic Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Pakistan_Economic_Corridor), allowing trains to depart Karachi and travel on Pakistani railways at an average speed of 160 km/h (100 mph) versus the current average speed of 80 km/h (50 mph).[^cite_note-266]

### Public transport

#### Metrobus

Main article: [Karachi Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metrobus)



A [Green line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_-_Karachi_Metrobus) Station

The Pakistani Government is developing the [Karachi Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metrobus) project, which is a 6-line 150-kilometre (93+1⁄4-mile) [bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit) system.[^cite_note-267] The Metrobus project was inaugurated by then-Prime Minister [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif) on 25 February 2016. Sharif said the "project will be more beautiful than Lahore Metro Bus".[^cite_note-268] Orange and Green Lines are operational while Red-Line is underconstruction.

#### People's Bus Service



Peoples Bus Service (Red)



Peoples Bus Service (Pink)

In 2022, the provincial government launched [Peoples Bus Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_Bus_Service) with a fleet of 100+ buses running on 12 different routes on nominal fare. The buses are air-conditioned, have [Wi-Fi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wi-Fi), have priority seating for the disabled and elderly, and are wheelchair accessible.

Red buses are for the general public. Pink buses are for [women only](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women-only_space). White buses are environment friendly electric buses having designated charging points.

#### Karachi Circular Railway

[Karachi Circular Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Circular_Railway) is a partially active regional [public transit system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport) in Karachi, which serves the [Karachi metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_District). KCR was fully operational between 1969 and 1999. Since 2001, restoration of the railway and restarting the system has been sought.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] In November 2020, the KCR partially revived operations.[^cite_note-271]

KCR was included in [CPEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China-Pakistan_Economic_Corridor) by [Shehbaz Sharif](6502561.md) and construction started in 2022. Existing 43 km KCR track and stations would be completely rebuilt into [automated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automated) [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) system with [electric trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_locomotive). The route would not be changed however many underpasses and bridges would be built along the route to eliminate 22-level crossings. New KCR would be similar to [Lahore](125315.md)'s [Orange Train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Line_\(Lahore_Metro\)). New KCR would have joint stations with [Karachi Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Breeze) at points of intersection. Project would be operational by 2025.

With its hub at [Karachi City station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_City_railway_station) on [I. I. Chundrigar Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._I._Chundrigar_Road), KCR will connect the city centre with several industrial, commercial and residential districts within the city.[^cite_note-272]

#### Tramway service

A [tramway](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special\:Search/tramway) service was started in 1884 in Karachi but was closed in 1975.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] However, the revival of tramway service is proposed by Karachi Administrator Iftikhar Ali. Turkey has offered assistance in the revival and launching modern tramway service in Karachi.[^cite_note-275]

### Air

Karachi's [Jinnah International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_International_Airport) is the busiest airport of Pakistan with a total of 7.2 million passengers in 2018. The current terminal structure was built in 1992, and is divided into international and domestic sections. Karachi's airport serves as a [hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_hub) for the [flag carrier](465816.md), [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines) (PIA), as well as for [Air Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Indus), [Serene Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SereneAir) and [airblue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airblue). The airport offers non-stop flights to destinations throughout East Asia, South Asia, Southeast Asia, Central Asia, the [Gulf States](3088555.md), Europe and North America.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277]

### Sea

The largest shipping ports in Pakistan are the [Port of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Karachi) and the nearby [Port Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Qasim), the former being the oldest port of Pakistan. Port Qasim is located 35 kilometres (22 miles) east of the Port of Karachi on the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) estuary. These ports handle 95% of Pakistan's trade cargo to and from foreign ports. These seaports have modern facilities which include bulk handling, containers and oil terminals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobert_StimsonKingsley_E._Haynes201243-278] The ports are part of the Maritime [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road).[^cite_note-279]

## Civic administration

Main articles: [Politics of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Karachi), [List of mayors of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Karachi), [List of Union Councils of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Union_Councils_of_Karachi), and [Commissioner of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioner_of_Karachi)

### City government

Main article: [Government of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Karachi)

Karachi has a fragmented system of civic government. The urban area is divided into six District Municipal Corporations: [Karachi East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_East_District), [Karachi West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_West_District), [Karachi Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Central_District), [Karachi South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_South_District), [Malir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_District), [Korangi.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korangi_District) Each district is further divided into between 22 and 42 Union Committees. Each Union Committee is represented by seven elected representatives, four of whom can be general candidates of any background; the other three seats are reserved for women, religious minorities, and a union representative or peasant farmer.

Karachi's urban area also includes six [cantonments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonment_\(Pakistan\)), which are administered directly by the [Pakistani military](23369.md), and include some of Karachi's most desirable real-estate.

Key civic bodies, such as the [Karachi Water and Sewerage Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Water_and_Sewerage_Board) and KBCA (Karachi Building Control Authority), among others, are under the direct control of the [Government of Sindh](3584314.md).[^cite_note-280] Additionally, Karachi's city-planning authority for undeveloped land, the [Karachi Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Development_Authority), is under control of the government, while two new city-planning authorities, the [Lyari Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari_Development_Authority) and [Malir Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_Development_Authority) were revived by the [Pakistan Peoples Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Peoples_Party) government in 2011 – allegedly to patronize their electoral allies and voting banks.[^cite_note-Hasan-281]

#### Historical background

In response to a cholera epidemic in 1846, the [Karachi Conservancy Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Conservancy_Board) was organized by British administrators to control its spread.[^cite_note-cdgkhistory-282][^cite_note-283] The board became the Karachi Municipal Commission in 1852, and the [Karachi Municipal Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Municipal_Committee) the following year.[^cite_note-cdgkhistory-282] The City of Karachi Municipal Act of 1933 transformed the city administration into the [Karachi Municipal Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Municipal_Corporation) with a mayor, a deputy mayor and 57 councillors.[^cite_note-cdgkhistory-282] In 1976, the body became the [Karachi Metropolitan Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metropolitan_Corporation).[^cite_note-cdgkhistory-282]

During the 1900s, Karachi saw its major beautification project under the mayoralty of [Harchandrai Vishandas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harchandrai_Vishandas). New roads, parks, residential, and recreational areas were developed as part of this project. In 1948, the [Federal Capital Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Capital_Territory_\(Pakistan\)) of Pakistan was created, comprising approximately 2,103 km<sup>2</sup> (812 sq mi) of Karachi and surrounding areas, but this was merged into the province of [West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Pakistan) in 1959.[^cite_note-284] In 1960, Karachi and Lasbela District merged to create Karachi-Bela Division. In 1972, [Lasbela District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lasbela_District) transferred to [Kalat Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalat_Division) and [Karachi metropolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metropolitan_Corporation) area was divided into three (03) districts [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_East_District), [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_West_District) and [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_South_District). In 1996, again the [Karachi metropolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metropolitan_Corporation) area was divided into More two (02) districts [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Central_District) and [Malir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_District), each with its own municipal corporation.[^cite_note-cdgkhistory-282]

#### Union councils (2001–11)



Given the honorary title "Father of Service", [Naimatullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naimatullah_Khan) Advocate (2001–2005) was one of the most successful and respected mayors Karachi ever had.

In 2001, during the rule of [General Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf), five districts of Karachi were merged to form the city district of Karachi, with a three-tier structure. The two most local tiers are composed of 18 [towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehsil), and 178 [union councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_councils_of_Pakistan).[^cite_note-285] Each tier focused on elected councils with some common members to provide "vertical linkage" within the federation.[^cite_note-286]

[Naimatullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naimatullah_Khan) was the first Nazim of Karachi during the Union Council period, while Shafiq-Ur-Rehman Paracha was the first district coordination officer of Karachi. [Syed Mustafa Kamal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Mustafa_Kamal) was elected City Nazim of Karachi to succeed Naimatullah Khan in 2005 elections, and [Nasreen Jalil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasreen_Jalil) was elected as the City Naib Nazim.

Each Union Council had thirteen members elected from specified electorates: four men and two women elected directly by the general population; two men and two women elected by peasants and workers; one member for minority communities; two members are elected jointly as the Union Mayor (*Nazim*) and Deputy Union Mayor (*Naib Nazim*).[^cite_note-287] Each council included up to three council secretaries and a number of other civil servants. The Union Council system was dismantled in 2011.

#### District Municipal Corporations (2011–2021)

In July 2011, city district government of Karachi was reverted its original constituent units known as District Municipal Corporations (DMC). The five original DMCs are: [Karachi East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_East_District), [Karachi West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_West_District), [Karachi Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Central_District), [Karachi South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_South_District) and [Malir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_District). In November 2013, a sixth DMC, [Korangi District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korangi_District) was carved out from District East.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] In August 2020, Sindh cabinet approves formation of the seventh district in Karachi (Keamari District), Keamari District was formed by splitting District West.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

The committees for each district devise and enforce land-use and zoning regulations within their district. Each committee also manages water supply, sewage, and roads (except for 28 main arteries, which are managed by the [Karachi Metropolitan Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metropolitan_Corporation)).[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] Street lighting, traffic planning, markets regulations, and signage are also under the control of the DMCs. Each DMC also maintains its own municipal record archive, and devises its own local budget.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102]

Municipal Administration of Karachi is also run by the [Karachi Metropolitan Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Metropolitan_Corporation) (KMC), which is responsible for the development and maintenance of main arteries, bridges, drains, several hospitals, beaches, solid waste management, as well as some parks, and the city's firefighting services.[^cite_note-297] Between 2016 and 2020 the mayor of Karachi was [Waseem Akhtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waseem_Akhtar) (2016–2020), with Arshad Hassan serving as Deputy Mayor; both served as part of the KMC. The Administrator of Karachi is Syed Saif-ur-Rehman as of 2022.[^cite_note-298] In 2023, [Murtaza Wahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtaza_Wahab) of [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_People's_Party) was elected the [mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Karachi) of Karachi.[^cite_note-dawn-9][^cite_note-299]

The position of [Commissioner of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioner_of_Karachi) was created, with Iftikhar Ali Shallwani serving this role.[^cite_note-300] There are six [military cantonments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonments_\(Pakistan\)), which are administered by the [Pakistani Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Army), and are some of Karachi's most upscale neighbourhoods.

| **Districts** **[Karachi East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_East)** **[Karachi West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_West)** **[Karachi South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_South)** **[Karachi Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Central)** **[Malir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_District)** **[Korangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korangi_District)** **[Kemari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemari_District)** |  | **Cantonments** A. [Karachi Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment) B. [Clifton Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton_Cantonment) C. [Korangi Creek Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korangi_Creek_Cantonment) D. [Faisal Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Cantonment) E. [Malir Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_Cantonment) F. [Manora Cantonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manora_Cantonment) |

#### District Municipal Corporations (2021–present)

26 Town Municipal Corporations were established in Karachi under the Sindh Local Government Act, 2021.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] One of these, **Moriro Mirbahar Town**, is named after [Moriro Mirbahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriro_ain_Mangermachh), a legendary hero from [Sindhi folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_folklore).[^cite_note-303]

### City planning

The [Karachi Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Development_Authority) (KDA), along with the [Lyari Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari_Development_Authority) (LDA) and [Malir Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malir_Development_Authority) (MDA), is responsible for the development of most undeveloped land around Karachi. KDA came into existence in 1957 with the task of managing land around Karachi, while the LDA and MDA were formed in 1993 and 1994, respectively. KDA under the control of Karachi's local government and mayor in 2001, while the LDA and MDA were abolished. KDA was later placed under the direct control of the [Government of Sindh](3584314.md) in 2011. The LDA and MDA were also revived by the [Pakistan Peoples Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Peoples_Party) government at the time, allegedly to patronize their electoral allies and voting banks.[^cite_note-Hasan-281] City-planning in Karachi, therefore, is not locally directed but is instead controlled at the provincial level.

Each District Municipal Corporation regulate land-use in developed areas, while the Sindh Building Control Authority ensures that building construction is in accordance with building & town planning regulations. Cantonment areas, and the [Defence Housing Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Housing_Authority,_Karachi) are administered and planned by the military.

## Municipal services

### Water

Municipal water supplies are managed by the Karachi Water and Sewerage Board (KW\&SB), which supplies 640 million gallons daily (MGD) to the city (excluding the city's steel mills and [Port Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Qasim)), of which 440 MGD are filtered/treated.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] Most of the supply comes from the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River), and 90 MGD from the [Hub Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hub_Dam).[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] Karachi's water supply is transported to the city through a complex network of canals, conduits, and siphons, with the aid of pumping and filtration stations.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] 80% of Karachi households have access to piped water as of 2022,[^cite_note-SPDC-304] with private water tankers supplying much of the water required in informal settlements.[^cite_note-Pakistan_Development_Update-2016-149] 15% of residents in a 2022 survey rated their water supply as "bad" or "very bad", while 40% expressed concern at the stability of water supply.[^cite_note-SPDC-304] By 2022, an estimated 35,000 people were dying due to water-borne diseases annually.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYamini_Narayanan2015165-305]

The **[K-IV water project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-IV_water_project)** is under development at a cost of \$876 million. It would connect [Keenjhar Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keenjhar_Lake) to Karachi hence eradicating water scarcity in eastern and northern parts of the city. It is expected to supply 650 million gallons daily of potable water to the city, the first phase 260 million gallons upon completion.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307]

Desalination plants are also planned to be built on Arabian Sea coast on western side of Karachi in near future. These would resolve water scarcity issues in western parts of the city including SITE Area, Shershah and Orangi Town.

### Sanitation

98% of Karachi's households are connected to the city's underground public sewerage system,[^cite_note-SPDC-304] largely operated by the Karachi Water and Sewerage Board (KW\&SB). The KW\&SB operates 150 pumping stations, 25 bulk reservoirs, over 10,000 kilometres of pipes, and 250,000 manholes.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] The city generates approximately 472 million gallons daily (MGD) of sewage, of which 417 MGD are discharged without treatment.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] KW\&SB has the optimum capacity to treat up to 150 MGD of sewage, but uses only about 50 MGD of this capacity.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] Three treatment plants are available, in [SITE Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SITE_Town) (Gutter Baghicha), [Mehmoodabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmudabad,_Karachi), and Mauripur.[^cite_note-shehri.org-2018-102] 75% reported in 2022 that Karachi's drainage system overflows or backs up,[^cite_note-SPDC-304] the highest percentage of all major Pakistani cities.[^cite_note-SPDC-304] Parts of the city's drainage system overflow on average 2–7 times per month, flooding some city streets.[^cite_note-SPDC-304]

Households in [Orangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orangi) self-organized to set-up their own sewerage system under the [Orangi Pilot Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orangi_Pilot_Project),[^cite_note-sewer-308] a community service organization founded in 1980. 90% of Orangi streets are now connected to a sewer system built by local residents under the Orangi Pilot Project.[^cite_note-sewer-308] Residents of individual streets bear the cost of sewerage pipes, and provide volunteer labour to lay the pipe.[^cite_note-sewer-308] Residents also maintain the sewer pipes,[^cite_note-sewer-308] while the city municipal administration has built several primary and secondary pipes for the network.[^cite_note-sewer-308] As a result of OPP, 96% of Orangi residents have access to a latrine.[^cite_note-sewer-308]

The [Sindh Solid Waste Management Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindh_Solid_Waste_Management_Board?action=edit\&redlink=1) (SSWMB) is responsible for the collection and disposal of solid waste, not only in Karachi but throughout the whole province. Karachi has the highest percentage of residents in Pakistan who report that their streets are never cleaned – 42% of residents in Karachi report their streets are never cleaned, compared to 10% of residents in [Lahore](125315.md).[^cite_note-SPDC-304] Only 17% of Karachi residents reporting daily street cleaning, compared to 45% in Lahore.[^cite_note-SPDC-304] 69% of Karachi residents rely on private garbage collection services,[^cite_note-SPDC-304] with only 15% relying on municipal garbage collection services.[^cite_note-SPDC-304] 53% of Karachi residents in a 2022 survey reported that the state of their neighbourhood's cleanliness was either "bad" or "very bad".[^cite_note-SPDC-304] compared to 35% in [Lahore](125315.md),[^cite_note-SPDC-304] and 16% in [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan).[^cite_note-SPDC-304]

### Electricity

The one and only electricity providing company in Karachi is [K-Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-Electric). It was government owned but was privatised in 2019. Government still has some shares. However [HUBCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HUBCO) is an Independent Power Producer (IPP) that owns few major powerplants.

Karachi mostly gets electricity from oil, gas and coal powerplants established either on western coastline or [Port Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Qasim) Industrial Zone. Most recently built coal powerplants were the 1320MW Port Qasim Powerplant and the 1320MW Hub Coal Powerplant. 3 Nuclear Powerplants on western coastline namely [KANUPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Nuclear_Power_Complex) (K-1, K-2, K-3) also feed Karachi. [Jhimpir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jhimpir), a nearby town has Wind Powerplants of more than 1000MW. This capacity is going to increase in future expansions. Solar Parks are envisioned to be established on western coastline having a starting generation of 1000MW.

75% of Karachi receives uninterrupted power supply almost throughout the year. 25% areas including industrial areas suffer with up to 6 hours of [power outages](296636.md) everyday due to energy generation deficit. Power outages increase further in Peak-summer and [Monsoon](57630.md) season (May to August). Many [slums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum) and unregulated areas are not yet electrified hence they indulge in electricity theft which is locally called Kunda-System.

### Police, Ambulance, Firefighting

Police is under the control of provincial government and city government has no authority over it. Ambulance is run by private hospitals or NGOs, the most famous of which are Edhi, Chhipa and JDC. Firefighting is under control of local government and has enough firefighters and vehicles to work quickly during fire. Less than 1% of all firefighters in Pakistan are women.[^cite_note-309]

## Education

Main article: [Education in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Karachi)



[Bai Virbaijee Soparivala (B.V.S.) Parsi High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B._V._S._Parsi_High_School)



[FAST NUCES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_of_Computer_and_Emerging_Sciences) Karachi campus

### Primary and secondary

See also: [List of schools in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Karachi)

Karachi's primary education system is divided into five levels: primary (grades one through five); [middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_school) (grades six through eight); high (grades nine and ten, leading to the [Secondary School Certificate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_School_Certificate)); [intermediate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) (grades eleven and twelve, leading to a Higher Secondary School Certificate); and university programs leading to [graduate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undergraduate_education) and [advanced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graduate_school) degrees. Karachi has both public and private educational institutions. Most educational institutions are gender-based from primary to intermediate. Universities are mostly co-education.

Several of Karachi's schools, such as [St Patrick's High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Patrick's_High_School,_Karachi), [St Joseph's Convent School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Joseph's_Convent_School,_Karachi) and [St Paul's English High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Paul's_English_High_School), are operated by Christian churches, and are among Pakistan's most prestigious schools.

### Higher

See also: [List of colleges in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_in_Karachi), [List of universities in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Karachi), and [List of medical schools in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_medical_schools_in_Karachi)



The [D. J. Sindh Government Science College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._J._Sindh_Government_Science_College) is one of Karachi's oldest universities and dates from 1887.



Karachi University is the city's largest by number of students, number of departments & occupied land area.

Karachi is home to several major public universities. Karachi's first public university's date from the [British colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) era. The [Sindh Madressatul Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindh_Madressatul_Islam_University) was founded in 1885 and was granted university status in 2012. Establishment of the Sindh Madressatul Islam was followed by the establishment of the [D. J. Sindh Government Science College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._J._Sindh_Government_Science_College) in 1887, which was granted university status in 2014. The [Nadirshaw Edulji Dinshaw University of Engineering and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NED_University_of_Engineering_and_Technology) (NED), was founded in 1921, and is Pakistan's oldest institution of higher learning. The [Dow University of Health Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dow_University_of_Health_Sciences) was established in 1945, and is now one of Pakistan's top medical research institutions.

The [University of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Karachi), founded in 1951, is Pakistan's largest university with a student population of 24,000. The [Institute of Business Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Business_Administration,_Karachi) (IBA), founded in 1955, is the oldest business school outside of North America and Europe, and was set up with technical support from the [Wharton School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharton_School) and the [University of Southern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_California). The [Dawood University of Engineering and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawood_University_of_Engineering_and_Technology), which opened in 1962, offers degree programmes in petroleum, gas, chemical, and industrial engineering. The [Pakistan Navy Engineering College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Navy_Engineering_College) (PNEC), operated by the [Pakistan Navy](1183789.md), is associated with the [National University of Sciences and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_of_Sciences_and_Technology,_Pakistan) (NUST) in Islamabad.

Karachi is also home to numerous private universities. The [Aga Khan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aga_Khan_University), founded in 1983, is Karachi's oldest private educational institution, and is one of Pakistan's most prestigious medical schools. The [Indus Valley School of Art and Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_School_of_Art_and_Architecture) was founded in 1989, and offers degree programmes in arts and architectural fields. [Hamdard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamdard_University) is the largest private university in Pakistan with faculties including Eastern Medicine, Medical, Engineering, Pharmacy, and Law. The [National University of Computer and Emerging Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_of_Computer_and_Emerging_Sciences) (NUCES-FAST), one of Pakistan's top universities in computer education, operates two campuses in Karachi. [Bahria University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahria_University) (BU) founded in 2000, is one of the major general institutions of Pakistan with their campuses in Karachi, Islamabad and Lahore offers degree programs in Management Sciences, Electrical Engineering, Computer Science and Psychology. [Sir Syed University of Engineering and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Syed_University_of_Engineering_and_Technology) (SSUET) offers degree programmes in biomedical, electronics, telecom and computer engineering. [Karachi Institute of Economics & Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Institute_of_Economics_&_Technology) (KIET) has two campuses in Karachi. The [Shaheed Zulfiqar Ali Bhutto Institute of Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaheed_Zulfiqar_Ali_Bhutto_Institute_of_Science_and_Technology) (SZABIST), founded in 1995 by former Prime Minister [Benazir Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benazir_Bhutto), operates a campus in Karachi.&#x20;

Other Karachi universities include:

- [Iqra University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iqra_University)

- [Habib University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habib_University)

- [Dow University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dow_University_of_Health_Sciences)

- [Jinnah Medical and Dental College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_Medical_and_Dental_College)

- [Jinnah Sindh Medical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_Sindh_Medical_University)

- [Karachi Institute of Economics and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Institute_of_Economics_and_Technology)

- [United Medical and Dental College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Medical_and_Dental_College)

- [Liaquat National Medical College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaquat_National_Medical_College)

- [Institute of Cost & Management Accountants of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Cost_&_Management_Accountants_of_Pakistan) (ICMAP)

- [Institute of Business Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Business_Management,_Karachi) (CBM)

## Healthcare

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of hospitals in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Karachi) and [Environment of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Karachi).

- Aga Khan University's hospital

  Aga Khan University's hospital

- Lady Dufferin Hospital

  Lady Dufferin Hospital

Karachi is a centre of research in biomedicine with at least 30 public hospitals, 80 registered private hospitals and 12 recognized medical colleges,[^cite_note-312] including the Indus Hospital, [Lady Dufferin Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_Dufferin_Hospital), [Karachi Institute of Heart Diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Institute_of_Heart_Diseases?action=edit\&redlink=1),[^cite_note-313] [National Institute of Cardiovascular Diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Cardiovascular_Diseases,_Pakistan),[^cite_note-314] [Civil Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Hospital),[^cite_note-315] [Combined Military Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Military_Hospital),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIssues_in_National,_Regional,_and_Environmental_Health_and_Medicine2013-316] [PNS Rahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PNS_Rahat),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEndocrine_System_Diseases:_New_Insights_for_the_Healthcare_Professional2013-317] [PNS Shifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PNS_Shifa),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZohra_ZaidiS.W_Lanigan2010-318] [Aga Khan University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aga_Khan_University_Hospital,_Karachi), [Liaquat National Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaquat_National_Hospital), [Jinnah Postgraduate Medical Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_Postgraduate_Medical_Centre),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMohammad_Aslam_UqailiKhanji_Harijan2011-319] [Holy Family Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Family_Hospital,_Karachi)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHelen_Renaux2011-320] and [Ziauddin Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziauddin_Hospital). In 1995, Ziauddin Hospital was the site of Pakistan's first bone marrow transplant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKenneth_D._MillerMiklos_Simon2015-321]

Karachi municipal authorities in 2017 launched a new early warning system that alerted city residents to a forecasted heatwave. Previous heatwaves had routinely claimed lives in the city, but implementation of the warning system was credited for no reported heat-related fatalities.[^cite_note-heatwave-136] During the 2020–2021 [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), vaccines were available in all major hospitals.

Further information: [Richmond Crawford Veterinary Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond_Crawford_Veterinary_Hospital)

## Entertainment, tourism and culture

Main article: [Culture of Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Karachi)

See also: [Culture of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Pakistan), [Muhajir culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajir_culture), and [Sindhi culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_culture)

### Shopping malls

Main article: [Cinema in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_in_Karachi)

Karachi is home to Pakistan and South Asia's largest shopping mall, [Lucky One Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LuckyOne_Mall) which hosts more than two hundred stores.[^cite_note-322] According to TripAdvisor the city is also home to Pakistan's favorite shopping mall, [Dolmen Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmen_Malls), [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Karachi) which was also featured on [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN_International).[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] In 2023, another mega mall/entertainment complex named 'Mall of Karachi' situated at the bottom of Pakistan's tallest skyscraper [Bahria Icon Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahria_Icon_Tower) will be opened.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326]

### Museums and galleries

- The famous "Priest-King" statue of the Indus Valley civilization is displayed at Karachi's National Museum of Pakistan.

  The famous "Priest-King" statue of the [Indus Valley civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilization) is displayed at Karachi's [National Museum of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Pakistan).

- Built as a home for a wealthy Hindu businessman, the Mohatta Palace is now a museum open to the public.

  Built as a home for a wealthy Hindu businessman, the [Mohatta Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasr-e-Fatima) is now a museum open to the public.

Karachi has several important museums. The [National Museum of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Pakistan) and [Mohatta Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasr-e-Fatima) exhibit a rich collection of artwork, while the city boasts several private art galleries.[^cite_note-327] Additionally, Karachi features the [Pakistan Airforce Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PAF_Museum,_Karachi), the [Pakistan Maritime Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Maritime_Museum) and the country's first interactive science centre, the [MagnifiScience Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MagnifiScience_Centre).[^cite_note-328] [Wazir Mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_Mansion), the birthplace of Pakistan's founder [Muhammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah) has also been preserved as a museum open to the public. [Quaid-e-Azam House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaid-e-Azam_House), the residence of [Muhammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah)'s residence, also serves as a museum showcasing his furniture and other belongings. Other museums include [TDF Ghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TDF_Ghar) and the [State Bank of Pakistan Museum & Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Bank_of_Pakistan_Museum_&_Art_Gallery).

### Theatre and cinema

Karachi is home to some of Pakistan's important cultural institutions. The [National Academy of Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Performing_Arts),[^cite_note-329] located in the former [Hindu Gymkhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Gymkhana,_Karachi), offers diploma courses in performing arts including classical music and contemporary theatre. Karachi is home to groups such as Thespianz Theater, a professional youth-based, non-profit performing arts group, which works on theatre and arts activities in Pakistan.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331]

Though [Lahore](125315.md) was considered to be home of Pakistan's film industry, Karachi is home to Urdu cinema and [Kara Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Film_Festival) annually showcases independent Pakistani and international films and documentaries.[^cite_note-332]

Bambino Cinema, Capri Cinema, Cinepax Cinema, Cinegold Plex Cinema (Bahria Town), Mega Multiplex Cinema (Millennium Mall), Nueplex Cinema (Askari-4), Atrium Mall Cinema (Sadar) are some of the most popular cinemas in Karachi.

### Music

The [All Pakistan Music Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Pakistan_Music_Conference), linked to the 45-year-old similar institution in [Lahore](125315.md), has been holding its annual music festival since its inception in 2004.[^cite_note-333] The National Arts Council (*Koocha-e-Saqafat*) has musical performances and [mushaira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mushaira).

## Social issues

### Crime and Lawlessness

Sometimes stated to be amongst the world's most dangerous cities,[^cite_note-334] the extent of violent crime in Karachi is not as significant in magnitude as compared to other cities.[^cite_note-Jaffrelot-2015-335] According to the Numbeo Crime Index 2014, Karachi was the 6th most dangerous city in the world. By the middle of 2016, Karachi's rank had dropped to 31 following the launch of anti-crime operations.[^cite_note-336] By 2018, Karachi's ranking has dropped to 50.[^cite_note-337] In 2021, Karachi's ranking fell to 115. In 2022, the ranking fell further to 128th place, ranking Karachi safer than regional cities such as [Dhaka](56656.md) (56th place), Delhi (90th place), and [Bangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangalore) (122nd place).[^cite_note-338]

The city's large population results in high numbers of homicides with a moderate homicide rate.[^cite_note-Jaffrelot-2015-335] Karachi's homicide rates are lower than many Latin American cities,[^cite_note-Jaffrelot-2015-335] and in 2015 was 12.5 per 100,000[^cite_note-339] – lower than the homicide rate of several American cities such as [New Orleans](53842.md) and [St. Louis](27687.md).[^cite_note-businessinsider.com-340] The homicide rates in some Latin American cities such as [Caracas](55289.md), [Venezuela](32374.md) and [Acapulco](1446.md), Mexico are in excess of 100 per 100,000 residents,[^cite_note-businessinsider.com-340] many times greater than Karachi's homicide rate. In 2016, the number of murders in Karachi had dropped to 471, which had dropped further to 381 in 2017.[^cite_note-The_Frontier_Post-2018-341]

In the late 1980s and early 1990s, Karachi was rocked by political conflict while crime rates drastically increased with the arrival of weaponry from the [War in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(1978%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-violentEnd-58] Several of Karachi's criminal mafias became powerful during a period in the 1990s described as "the rule of the mafias."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarcello_Balbo2005181-342] Major mafias active in the city included land mafia, water tanker mafia, transport mafia and a sand and gravel mafia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENewsline2006-343][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarcello_Balbo2005181-342][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMohammad_Shahid_Alam199649-344][^cite_note-345] Karachi's highest death rates occurred in the mid-1990s. In 1995, 1,742 killings were recorded, with a maximum of 15 killings in a single day.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347]

#### Karachi Operation by Pakistan Rangers

Karachi had become widely known for its high rates of violent crime, but rates sharply decreased following a controversial crackdown operation against criminals, the MQM political party, and Islamist militants initiated in 2013 by the [Pakistan Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Rangers).[^cite_note-New_York_TImes-59] In 2015, 1,040 Karachiites were killed in either acts of [terror](2816470.md) or other crime – an almost 50% decrease from the 2,023 killed in 2014,[^cite_note-348] and an almost 70% decrease from the 3,251 recorded killed in 2013 – the highest ever recorded number in Karachi history.[^cite_note-349] Violent crime like target killings, kidnappings for ransom or extortion, burning or torturing to death, drugs and weapons smuggling decreased sharply after 2015. Street crime still remains high like snatching of cash, phones, motorcycles and cars on gunpoint.[^cite_note-350]

With 650 [homicides](123451.md) in 2015, Karachi's homicide rate decreased by 75% compared to 2013.[^cite_note-Reuters-351] In 2017, the number of homicides had dropped further to 381.[^cite_note-The_Frontier_Post-2018-341] Extortion crimes decreased by 80% between 2013 and 2015, while kidnappings decreased by 90% during the same period.[^cite_note-Reuters-351] By 2016, the city registered a total of 21 cases of kidnap for ransom.[^cite_note-352] Terrorist incidents dropped by 98% between 2012 and 2017, according to Pakistan's Interior Ministry.[^cite_note-353] As a result of the Karachi's improved security environment, real-estate prices in Karachi rose sharply after 2015,[^cite_note-354] with a rise in business for upmarket restaurants and cafés.[^cite_note-355]

### Ethnic and linguistic conflict

Insufficient affordable housing infrastructure to absorb growth has resulted in the city's diverse migrant populations being largely confined to ethnically homogeneous neighbourhoods.[^cite_note-Pakistan_Development_Update-2016-149] The 1970s saw major [labour struggles in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_labour_unrest_of_1972)'s industrial estates. Violence originated in the city's university campuses, and spread into the city.[^cite_note-356] Conflict was especially sharp between [MQM party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muttahida_Qaumi_Movement_%E2%80%93_Pakistan) and ethnic [Sindhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhis), [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns), and [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabis). The party and its vast network of supporters were targeted by Pakistani security forces as part of the controversial [Operation Clean-up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Clean-up) in 1992, as part of an effort to restore peace in the city that lasted until 1994. The ethnic conflicts kept going between linguistic groups until the late 2010s and are now less extreme.

### Poor infrastructure

Urban planning and service delivery have not kept pace with Karachi's growth, resulting in the city's low ranking on livability rankings.[^cite_note-Pakistan_Development_Update-2016-149] The city has no cohesive transportation policy and inadequate transport, though up to 1,000 new vehicles are added daily to the city's congested streets.[^cite_note-Pakistan_Development_Update-2016-149] Roads and streets are broken at many places but are not repaired in timely manner.

### Pollution

As a large metropolitan area, Karachi has many sources of pollution. Alongside air, water and noise pollution, waste management is a challenge due to solid trash not disposed to dedicated dumping sites.[^cite_note-357]

#### Air pollution

This section is an excerpt from [Air pollution in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution_in_Karachi).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Air_pollution_in_Karachi\&action=edit)]

Karachi is the largest city in Pakistan and the [12th largest city in the world ](14649921.md) is facing a significant air pollution problem.[^cite_note-358] The air quality of Karachi has been deteriorating over the years, with pollution levels often exceeding safe limits set by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO).[^cite_note-Air_pollution_in_Karachi_auto-359]

A 2025 analysis by [Climate TRACE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_TRACE) of the urban areas with the most people exposed to unhealthy levels of air pollution identified Karachi as the largest.[^cite_note-360]



Karachi from above

#### Water pollution

There is water pollution in Lyari and Malir rivers as gutters directly open into these rivers. These rivers then directly run into the Arabian Sea untreated. So sewerage and industrial wastewater is directly being thrown into Indian Ocean. Three waste water treatment plants exist but are all dysfunctional.[^cite_note-361]

Karachi's sewage system is overwhelmed and in disrepair, and sewage commonly gets into the city's water lines. Thousands of hospitalisations each year are attributed to sewage contamination in Karachi's drinking water, and there are annual [cholera](7591.md) outbreaks in the city. As of 2011, half of all middle-class children in Karachi have [intestinal parasites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intestinal_parasite_infection) attributed to poor water quality.[^cite_note-Pappas_2011-362]<sup>: 787 </sup>

### Urban flooding in monsoon season

Size of Drainage system and storm water drains (locally known as Naalahs) in the city is not enough to handle the heavy rainfalls of monsoon. The drainage system and storm water drains are also filled with solid trash. When water finds no path, it enters streets, roads, underpasses and even houses during rainfall in July and August of every year. Major Naalahs like Orangi Naalah, Gujjar Naalah, Mehmoodabad Naalah are cleaned every year by government but are polluted by people the next day.[^cite_note-363]

Flooding hinders connectivity of different areas of the city specially Landhi and Korangi. Floods have caused drown or electric shocks related deaths as well.[^cite_note-364]

## Architecture

See also: [Pakistani architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_architecture) and [List of tallest buildings in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Karachi)

Karachi has a collection of buildings and structures of varied [architectural styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_style). The downtown districts of [Saddar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddar) and [Clifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifton,_Karachi) contain early 20th-century architecture, ranging in style from the [neo-classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) [KPT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Port_Trust) building to the [Sindh High Court](11042310.md) Building. Karachi acquired its first neo-Gothic or Indo-Gothic buildings when [Frere Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frere_Hall), [Empress Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empress_Market) and [St. Patrick's Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Patrick's_Cathedral,_Karachi) were completed. The [Mock Tudor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mock_Tudor) architectural style was introduced in the [Karachi Gymkhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Gymkhana) and the [Boat Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Boat_Club). [Neo-Renaissance architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Renaissance_architecture) was popular in the 19th century and was the architectural style for St. Joseph's Convent (1870) and the [Sind Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sind_Club) (1883).[^cite_note-365] The classical style made a comeback in the late 19th century, as seen in [Lady Dufferin Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_Dufferin_Hospital) (1898)[^cite_note-366] and the [Cantt. Railway Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment_Railway_Station). While [Italianate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italianate) buildings remained popular, an eclectic blend termed [Indo-Saracenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Saracenic) or Anglo-Mughal began to emerge in some locations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaurent_Gayer201434-367] The local mercantile community began acquiring impressive structures. Zaibunnisa Street in the [Saddar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddar) area (known as Elphinstone Street in British days) is an example where the mercantile groups adopted the [Italianate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italianate) and [Indo-Saracenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Saracenic) style to demonstrate their familiarity with Western culture and their own. The [Hindu Gymkhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Gymkhana,_Karachi) (1925) and [Mohatta Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasr-e-Fatima) are examples of Mughal revival buildings.[^cite_note-368] The Sindh Wildlife Conservation Building, located in Saddar, served as a Freemasonic Lodge until it was taken over by the government. There are talks of it being taken away from this custody and being renovated and the Lodge being preserved with its original woodwork and ornate wooden staircase.[^cite_note-369]

[Indus Valley School of Art and Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_School_of_Art_and_Architecture) is one of the prime examples of Architectural conservation and restoration where an entire [Nusserwanjee building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusserwanjee_building) from [Kharadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharadar) area of Karachi has been relocated to Clifton for adaptive reuse in an art school. The procedure involved the careful removal of each piece of timber and stone, stacked temporarily, loaded on the trucks for transportation to the Clifton site, unloaded and re-arranged according to a given layout, stone by stone, piece by piece, and completed within three months.[^cite_note-370]

Architecturally distinctive, even eccentric, buildings have sprung up throughout Karachi. Notable example of contemporary architecture include the [Pakistan State Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_State_Oil) Headquarters building. The city has examples of modern [Islamic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture), including the [Aga Khan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aga_Khan_University) hospital, [Grand Jamia Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Jamia_Mosque,_Karachi), [Masjid e Tooba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_e_Tooba), Faran Mosque, [Baitul Mukarram Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baitul_Mukarram_Mosque), Quaid's Mausoleum, and the [Textile Institute of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_Institute_of_Pakistan). One of the unique cultural elements of Karachi is that the residences, which are two- or three-story [townhouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Townhouse), are built with the front yard protected by a high brick wall. [I. I. Chundrigar Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._I._Chundrigar_Road) features a range of tall buildings. The most prominent examples include the [Habib Bank Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habib_Bank_Plaza), UBL Tower, PRC Towers, PNSC Building and [MCB Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MCB_Tower).[^cite_note-371] Newer skyscrapers are being built in Clifton. At least 50 150m+ buildings were underconstruction in 2022.

## Sports

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of sports venues in Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sports_venues_in_Karachi).



The match held between [Sind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sind_Province_\(1936%E2%80%931955\)) & Australia in Karachi on 22 November 1935 was reported by *[The Sydney Morning Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sydney_Morning_Herald)*



The [National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stadium,_Karachi) in Karachi

### Cricket

[Cricket](25675557.md)'s history in Pakistan predates the creation of the country in 1947. The first ever international cricket match in Karachi was held on 22 November 1935 between [Sindh](28972.md) and Australian cricket teams. The match was seen by 5,000 Karachiites.[^cite_note-372] Karachi is also the place that innovated [tape ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tape_ball), a safer and more affordable alternative to cricket.[^cite_note-373]

The inaugural first-class match at the National Stadium was played between Pakistan and India on 26 February 1955 and since then Pakistani national cricket team has won 20 of the 41 [Test matches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Test_cricket) played at the National Stadium.[^cite_note-374] The first [One Day International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Day_International) at the National Stadium was against the West Indies on 21 November 1980, with the match going to the last ball.

The national team has been less successful in such limited-overs matches at the ground, including a five-year stint between 1996 and 2001, when they failed to win any matches. The city has been host to a number of domestic cricket teams including Karachi,[^cite_note-375] Karachi Blues,[^cite_note-376] Karachi Greens,[^cite_note-377] and Karachi Whites.[^cite_note-378] The National Stadium hosted two group matches (Pakistan v. South Africa on 29 February and Pakistan v. England on 3 March), and a quarter-final match (South Africa v. West Indies on 11 March) during the [1996 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Cricket_World_Cup).[^cite_note-379]

[Rafi Cricket Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafi_Cricket_Stadium) under construction in Bahria Town would soon become the largest cricket stadium in Pakistan with a capacity of 50,000+ spectators.[^cite_note-380]

### Football







Pakistan national football team in a friendly against [CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_CSKA_Moscow) from the Soviet Union at the KMC Stadium on 28 February 1969

[Lyari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyari), a neighbourhood in Karachi, holds an important place in Pakistan's [football](10568.md) landscape due to its historical and cultural ties to the sport. Dating back several decades, Lyari has been a consistent source of football talent, contributing significantly to the national sports scene.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] One notable aspect is the nickname "Little Brazil" often associated with Lyari.[^cite_note-arab.news-383][^cite_note-384]

In its early years, football in Pakistan was mainly concentrated to Balochistan and the locality of Lyari,[^cite_note-385] from where majority of players of the [Pakistan national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_national_football_team) were recruited mainly in the 1960s, which is often regarded as the early [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age) of Pakistani football.[^cite_note-ah-386][^cite_note-fp-387] Notable players during this period include [Abdul Ghafoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Ghafoor_\(footballer\)), nicknamed the "Pakistani [Pelé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pel%C3%A9)" and "Black Pearl of Pakistan",[^cite_note-388] [Muhammad Umer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Umer), [Moosa Ghazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moosa_Ghazi), [Abid Ghazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abid_Ghazi), [Turab Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turab_Ali), [Ali Nawaz Baloch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Nawaz_Baloch), among others.[^cite_note-ah-386][^cite_note-fp-387] The Kakri Ground and [People's Football Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Football_Stadium), which is one of the major football stadiums in the country are located in the city.[^cite_note-389] In 2005, the city hosted the [2005 SAFF Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_SAFF_Gold_Cup_squads) at the Peoples Football Stadium, as well as the [Super Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Football_League) in 2007, which attracted capacity crowds during the games.

### Other sports

Some sources cite that it was in 1877 at Karachi, in what was then in British India ,where the first attempt was made to form a set of rules of for badminton.[^cite_note-390]

Karachi has hosted seven editions of the National Games of Pakistan, most recently in 2007.[^cite_note-391]

The popularity of golf is increasing, with clubs in Karachi like Dreamworld Resort, Bahria Town Golf Club, Hotel & Golf Club, Arabian Sea Country Club, DA Country & Golf Club. The city has facilities for [field hockey](10886.md) ([Hockey Club of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hockey_Club_of_Pakistan), UBL Hockey Ground), [boxing](4243.md) (KPT Sports Complex), [squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squash_\(sport\)) ([Jahangir Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahangir_Khan) Squash Complex), and [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo). There are marinas and boating clubs. [National Bank of Pakistan Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bank_of_Pakistan_Sports_Complex) is [First-class cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-class_cricket) venue and Multi-purpose sports facility in Karachi.

[^cite_note-214]: &#x20;Bhavnani, Nandita (2014). *The Making of Exile: Sindhi Hindus and the Partition of India*. Westland. pp. 39–40. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-93-84030-33-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-93-84030-33-9). In June 1947, it was initially proposed to settle the muhajirs on a large plot of land in Bunder Road Extension, a well-heeled suburb of Karachi. This was, however, a residential area dominated by affluent Sindhi Hindus, who became nervous about such a large number of discontented lower-class Muslim refugees living in such close proximity to them. Given their influence, the Hindus were able to sway the government into transferring the proposed resettlement site to Lyari, a more congested and lower middle-class area.

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-415-17297-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-415-17297-4). In 1947, as the new Federal Government of Pakistan struggled to establish itself in Karachi, a large number of Muslim refugees from northern India came and settled down in the city ... Karachi became the preferred destination of northern Indian Urdu-speaking Muslims who hoped to find white-collar employment opportunities in the cosmopolitan commercial and port city.
